Mexican President Andres Manuel Lopez Obrador swept into office in 2018 with the motto laying out his administration’s priorities: “For the good of all, first the poor.” (AP Photo/Marco Ugarte, File)\nAdd AP News on Google\nAdd AP News as your preferred source to see more of our stories on Google.\nShare\nShare\nFacebook\nCopy\nLink copied\nPrint\nEmail\nX\nLinkedIn\nBluesky\nFlipboard\nPinterest\nReddit\nRead More\nBy\nCHRISTOPHER SHERMAN\nUpdated [hour]:[minute] [AMPM] [timezone], [monthFull] [day], [year]\nAdd AP News on Google\nAdd AP News as your preferred source to see more of our stories on Google.\nShare\nShare\n\nFacebook\nCopy\nLink copied\nPrint\nEmail\nX\nLinkedIn\nBluesky\nFlipboard\nPinterest\nReddit\nMEXICO CITY (AP) — President\nAndrés Manuel López Obrador\nswept into office nearly six years ago with a simple motto laying out his administration’s priorities: “For the good of all, first the poor.”\nHis administration scrapped a host of existing social programs and installed their own, quickly increasing overall social spending to unprecedented heights for senior citizens, unemployed youth, students, farmers and people with disabilities.\nBut less noticed was that the new roster of social programs dramatically shifted who was getting that money. Suddenly, Mexico’s poorest citizens were receiving a smaller portion of the spending and less money than under previous administrations.\nMeanwhile, some of Mexico’s wealthiest started getting money they didn’t really need.\nFILE - Mexican President Andres Manuel Lopez Obrador deplanes after traveling in economy on a commercial flight from Guadalajara to Mexico City, March 9, 2019. (AP Photo/Marco Ugarte, File)\nFILE - Mexican President Andres Manuel Lopez Obrador deplanes after traveling in economy on a commercial flight from Guadalajara to Mexico City, March 9, 2019. (AP Photo/Marco Ugarte, File)\nAdd AP News on Google\nAdd AP News as your preferred source to see more of our stories on Google.\nShare\nShare\nFacebook\nCopy\n\nLink copied\nPrint\nEmail\nX\nLinkedIn\nBluesky\nFlipboard\nPinterest\nReddit\nRead More\nThe shift owed largely to a massive “universal” pension benefit for seniors that López Obrador launched on a chilly January day outside Mexico City in 2019, just weeks after taking office. He announced he was more than doubling the existing federal pension — it has since doubled again — and expanding it regardless of income to people who previously didn’t qualify, like those who received another pension from their former employer.\nIf much more money isn’t poured into the system, “universal programs spread benefits more thinly over the whole population with the result that the people who were most in need get worse,” said Robert Greenstein, a fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington. “Poverty can go up. Inequality can be greater than it would be under a more targeted scheme.”\nBut López Obrador’s social programs have proven so popular that even the opposition candidates running to replace him in the\nJune 2 election\nhave promised to expand them. Some 28 million Mexicans will benefit from one of the programs this year. In Sunday night’s final presidential debate, candidate\nXóchitl Gálvez\nsaid she would lower the minimum pension age to 60 from 65.\nFILE - A car covered with handicrafts for sale is parked on a street in Mexico City, Dec. 4, 2023. (AP Photo/Eduardo Verdugo, File)\nFILE - A car covered with handicrafts for sale is parked on a street in Mexico City, Dec. 4, 2023. (AP Photo/Eduardo Verdugo, File)\nAdd AP News on Google\nAdd AP News as your preferred source to see more of our stories on Google.\nShare\nShare\nFacebook\nCopy\n\nLink copied\nPrint\nEmail\nX\nLinkedIn\nBluesky\nFlipboard\nPinterest\nReddit\nRead More\nThe pension is the largest social program by budget in López Obrador’s slate of handouts, far surpassing the also well known Youth Building the Future, which pays young adults who neither study nor work to apprentice, and Sowing Life, which pays farmers to plant fruit or lumber-producing trees on their land.\nCombined with the elimination of predecessors’ more targeted programs that had focused on Mexico’s most in need, experts say the pension dramatically shifted the distribution of government funds.\nFour months from the end of López Obrador’s six-year term, several million people have escaped poverty. But factors beyond the social programs are involved, including López Obrador’s nearly tripling of the minimum wage and Mexicans abroad continuing to send home record amounts of money to relatives.\nCuriously, there are about 400,000 more Mexicans in extreme poverty than at the beginning of his term, according to government data.\nA government report published every two years that divides Mexico’s population into 10 segments by income says the very poorest segment in 2018 received about 19% of social spending. Just two years later, that poorest group was receiving only about 6%, said Manuel Martínez Espinoza, a researcher at Mexico’s National Council of the Humanities, Sciences and Technologies. For reasons unknown the government has not published the 2022 report.\nFILE - Trash collectors ride on their garbage truck in Mexico City, Tuesday, Oct. 6, 2020. (AP Photo/Marco Ugarte, File)\nFILE - Trash collectors ride on their garbage truck in Mexico City, Tuesday, Oct. 6, 2020. (AP Photo/Marco Ugarte, File)\nAdd AP News on Google\nAdd AP News as your preferred source to see more of our stories on Google.\nShare\nShare\nFacebook\nCopy\nLink copied\nPrint\n\nEmail\nX\nLinkedIn\nBluesky\nFlipboard\nPinterest\nReddit\nRead More\nCASH TO FAMILIES, BUT WITH A CATCH\nAt a counter in a central Mexico City market, Arturo García leaned over a steaming bowl of tripe stew on a recent morning. The 73-year-old retired cab driver said he stopped taking fares during the pandemic.\nNow the $362 (6,000 pesos) he receives from his universal pension every two months and some money he gets renting out a storage space in his home to street vendors are his only sources of income.\n“You have money or you don’t have money, they give it to you,” García said of the pension. “The government is trying to make us all equal.”\nOne of the programs López Obrador ended when he took office was called Prospera. It had targeted Mexico’s poorest families for some two decades under various names with what were known as conditional cash transfers. Poor families received money, but it was restricted by income level and recipients had to meet some requirements to get it, like taking their children for medical checkups.\nFILE - Mexican President Andres Manuel Lopez Obrador addresses the nation on his inauguration day at Congress in Mexico City, Dec. 1, 2018. (AP Photo/Eduardo Verdugo, File)\nFILE - Mexican President Andres Manuel Lopez Obrador addresses the nation on his inauguration day at Congress in Mexico City, Dec. 1, 2018. (AP Photo/Eduardo Verdugo, File)\nAdd AP News on Google\nAdd AP News as your preferred source to see more of our stories on Google.\nShare\nShare\nFacebook\nCopy\nLink copied\nPrint\nEmail\nX\n\nLinkedIn\nBluesky\nFlipboard\nPinterest\nReddit\nRead More\nThe president said the program was clientelist and suffered from systematic corruption, though instances of corruption have also been found in López Obrador’s programs.\nTargeted social programs like Prospera attempt to be more precise in steering public funds to specific segments of the population. For that reason they tend to be less expensive than universal programs.\nCritics, however, say they stigmatize the poor; have less political support, which makes them vulnerable to being cut; require more administration to determine eligibility and fewer people enroll, said Greenstein, the fellow at the Brookings Institution, adding that those risks are not inherent in targeted programs.\nMexico’s Welfare Ministry did not respond to requests for comment.\nProspera’s funding was redirected to López Obrador’s programs, principally the universal pension, signaling an important shift from a means-tested program that largely benefitted poor children to one that provided cash to all senior citizens.\nOne of the more cynical criticisms of the shift is that children don’t vote, but seniors do.\nPEOPLE WHO DON’T REALLY NEED IT ARE GETTING MORE\nPeople line up outside a branch of “Banco Bienestar,” or Welfare Bank, in Irapuato, Mexico, Thursday, Feb. 29, 2024. The bank was created by Mexican President Andres Manuel Lopez Obrador as a vehicle to get payments from his administration’s programs directly into the hands of Mexicans. (AP Photo/Fernando Llano)\nPeople line up outside a branch of “Banco Bienestar,” or Welfare Bank, in Irapuato, Mexico, Thursday, Feb. 29, 2024. The bank was created by Mexican President Andres Manuel Lopez Obrador as a vehicle to get payments from his administration’s programs directly into the hands of Mexicans. (AP Photo/Fernando Llano)\nAdd AP News on Google\nAdd AP News as your preferred source to see more of our stories on Google.\nShare\nShare\nFacebook\nCopy\nLink copied\nPrint\nEmail\nX\n\nLinkedIn\nBluesky\nFlipboard\nPinterest\nReddit\nRead More\nThe other side of Mexico’s poorest receiving a smaller proportion of social spending under this administration is that people who don’t really need it are getting more.\nOne morning in late April, César Herrera brought his elderly mother to a branch of the Banco Bienestar, or Welfare Bank, in Mexico City to withdraw her pension payment. The bank was created by López Obrador as a vehicle to get payments from his administration’s programs directly into the hands of Mexicans.\nHerrera said he and his mother had driven by in February when the last pension deposit was made and saw the line stretching down the street. But unlike many seniors who live payment to payment, Herrera said his mother didn’t need the money, so they left.\n“However, it’s there,” he said when they returned a month and a half later. “Of course you have to take it.”\nThe ninth out of 10 income strata, or the second highest, analyzed by the government went from receiving about $4.40 of every $100 in social spending in 2018 to getting about $10 in 2020, said Martínez, the researcher at the humanities, sciences and technologies council.\nMartínez said his field work in Chiapas, Mexico’s poorest state, found many people who were not receiving as much money as they had previously under Prospera, but who nevertheless fervently supported López Obrador.\nRelated Stories\nUS cites ‘unauthorized travel’ to end DACA status for a mother of 3 deported to Mexico\n3 MIN READ\n321\nMarlon James, Douglas Stuart and Elizabeth Strout are among Booker Prize semifinalists\n2 MIN READ\nTropical Storm Fausto to splash past Hawaii as Hurricane Genevieve sends swells to Mexico\n1 MIN READ\n“I’ve talked with a lot of people in my field work, they feel valued, they feel the president values them, which they didn’t feel before,” Martínez said.\nMartínez hypothesizes that the growth in extreme poverty during this administration was due in part to the elimination of Prospera but also the fact that people in extreme poverty tend to work in the informal sector — which would not have benefited equally from the increased minimum wage. Another factor was the COVID-19 pandemic forced a lot of families to exhaust their limited savings on health care.\nMuch of the back-and-forth between López Obrador’s anointed successor\nClaudia Sheinbaum\nand the opposition candidate Gálvez has been the president and Sheinbaum insisting that Gálvez will end the social programs if she wins — and Gálvez promising that she won’t. Much of that debate is unnecessary since the pension is now enshrined in the constitution.\n\nMartínez said that even at the current 65 minimum age the program is burning through public funds too rapidly.\n“In the short term, it’s a time bomb because it’s going to generate problems because it isn’t fiscally sustainable,” he said.\nMost read\nSpain deploys military to Ceuta after thousands of people breach Morocco’s border and at least 9 die\n5 MIN READ\n133\nThe Trump administration is ending a Medicare drug subsidy program. 31,\nhttps://www.ift.org.mx/sites/default/files/contenidogeneral/estadistica…\n.\nA5\n1.00-4.00 pts\n0-4 pts\nDo national regulatory bodies that oversee service providers and digital technology fail to operate in a free, fair, and independent manner?\n2.00\n2\n4.00\n4\nAs part of the 2013 constitutional reform, the government established IFT as a new autonomous regulatory agency to increase the transparency of media regulation.\n1\nIFT has the legal mandate to act as an antitrust body and protect the industry from monopolistic practices. The Federal Economic Competition Commission (COFECE) is another regulatory body that, in 2021, was recognized by the Federal Judiciary (PJF) as the competent authority to regulate the markets for online search engines, social networking, and cloud computing services.\n2\nHowever, under the López Obrador administration, IFT and other independent bodies continued to be targets of government pressure,\n\n3\nincluding sizable budget reductions.\n4\nIn February 2024, López Obrador introduced a series of reforms that included a proposal to eliminate seven independent entities, including COFECE, IFT, and the National Institute for Transparency, Access to Information, and Personal Data Protection (INAI).\n5\nIt remains unclear whether Claudia Sheinbaum, who assumed the presidency in October 2024, will enact these plans, though she has signaled her intention to do so.\n6\nThe López Obrador administration also hindered regulatory bodies with bureaucratic delays. President López Obrador declined to fill vacancies on the boards of IFT and COFECE in recent years, leaving them both without fully functioning boards. The bodies appealed to the SCJN,\n7\nand in November 2022, the SCJN ruled in favor of COFECE, ordering the executive branch to appoint commissioners.\n8\nLópez Obrador complied by appointing three new commissioners,\n9\nall of whom were ratified by the Senate.\n10\nThough civil society organizations have demanded that the SCJN provide a prompt response to IFT’s case,\n11\nIFT continued to function with only four of seven commissioners through the end of the coverage period.\n12\nThe president’s failure to name commissioners to the autonomous bodies has been considered detrimental to the institutions' independence and ability to function properly.\n13\nDespite receiving some criticism in recent years for its decisions on antitrust measures and draft net neutrality guidelines,\n14\nIFT has continued to reaffirm its independence. In May 2021, for instance, IFT challenged the creation of a biometric cell phone registry—one that Congress had directed the agency to create, operate, and maintain—as unconstitutional (see C4).\n15\n\nHowever, in February 2024, IFT was criticized for authorizing changes that allow CFE TEIT to provide nonprofit internet service in some zones already covered by other telecommunication operators, rather than only in areas where no coverage exists.\n16\nCritics of the decision claimed that IFT had caved to pressure from the executive branch.\n17\nPreviously, from 2019 to 2021, IFT was also criticized for its Traffic Management and Internet Administration Guidelines, which civil society actors claimed were an attempt to undermine net neutrality in Mexico (see B6).\n18\nNew guidelines went into effect in September 2021.\n19\n1\nJuan Montes, “Mexico Telecoms Reform Bill Advances,” The Wall Street Journal, March 22, 2013,\nhttp://on.wsj.com/1LXSc6E\n2\n“El Poder Judicial cumulative government support of 2.7 percent of 2021 GDP during 2019–2021, with PEMEX debt of about MXN 2.243 trillion at end-2021. The 2022 budget aimed to stabilize debt and deficits; PEMEX continued to report losses and high financial obligations and remained dependent on substantial government support.", "question_ids": ["5B.1", "5B.10", "5B.3", "5B.6", "5B.9", "8B.2", "8B.3", "8B.6"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The evidence concerns 2019–2022 and forecasts 2023; it is contextual rather than a direct 2023 execution finding.", "ruler_attribution": "The fiscal choices and PEMEX support are attributed to the government during López Obrador's administration.", "limitations": ["The document's 2023 figures are projections made in 2022, not observed 2023 outcomes."], "start_unit": 18, "end_unit": 20, "locator": "page 18 through page 20", "exact_excerpt": "The World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nThe average return on equity increased to 14.5 percent in 2021, after a sharp decline to 5.2 percent in\n2020 due to higher provisions.\n13. The authorities' countercyclical fiscal response remains cautious with public sector borrowing\nrequirements (PSBR) of 3.8 percent of GDP during 2021. Direct budgetary support in 2020 amounted to\n0.7 percent of GDP whereas guarantees and loans to MSMEs by development banks amounted to 1.2\npercent of GDP, focused on increased public health spending, small credits to MSMEs and the informal\nsector, and targeted social transfers to vulnerable groups. Mexico's overall PSBR were 4 percent of GDP\nin 2020, driven by a moderate increase in public spending and sustained revenues fueled by strong tax\nadministration measures and tax settlements with large companies. Yet, net public debt as a share of GDP\nincreased to 52.4 percent due to the fiscal deficit, debt reevaluation determined by foreign exchange\n(FOREX), and the large contraction in nominal GDP in 2020. The fiscal response during the pandemic, albeit\nat the risk of a deeper contraction and the sharp depreciation led to an increase in public indebtedness,\nbelow however to what was observed in other emerging markets. The 2021 fiscal situation benefited from\nthe economic recovery and higher oil prices, an expenditure that continues part of the pandemic fiscal\nstimulus centered on public health and social transfers and implementing large public investment\nprojects.\n14. Petróleos Mexicanos (PEMEX) has continued to report losses, equivalent to MXN 224 billion\n(US$10.9 billion) in 2021. Financial support from the Government during 2019–2021 (amounting to a\ncumulative 2.7 percent of 2021 GDP) and higher oil prices mitigated these losses. Federal government\nsupport to PEMEX came in the form of tax relief (US$12.7 billion), financing debt repayments (US$10\nbillion), and direct funding for investment projects (US$6.5 billion), and cash support for other obligations\n(US$6.7 billion), totaling US$35.9 billion (2.7 percent of GDP)18. This financial support was aimed to\npartially compensate for the US$41.6 billion losses of 2019 and 2020. PEMEX’s financial debt remains high\nand total financial obligations reached MXN 2243 billion (US$109 billion) at the end of 2021. PEMEX is\nexpected to benefit from the higher oil prices as the Mexican-Mix oil price averaged US$35.7 per barrel in\n2020, to reach US$64.7 in 2021, while averaging almost US$90 per barrel in the first quarter of 2022.\nPEMEX's oil production declined from an average of over 2 million barrels per day during 2015-2017, to\naround 1.75 million barrels per day in 2021. The fiscal support improves some of PEMEX challenges related\nto significant tax burden and high financial cost. A turnaround of PEMEX finances would also require to\nprioritize profitable business areas by redirecting investment for replacing reserves, scaling down large\ninvestment projects and focusing on maintaining and upgrading existing refineries, stimulating synergies\nwith the private sector in exploration and oil production, and selling noncore assets. PEMEX remains three\nnotches below investment grade for Fitch and Moody’s after a downgrade by Moody’s by one notch in\nJuly 2021 (it is still investment grade for S&P). The fiscal support to PEMEX as well as its balance and debt\nare part of the fiscal account figures reported for the consolidated public sector (tables 1 and 2).\n15. The 2022 public budget approved aims to stabilize the overall fiscal deficit at 2.7 percent of GDP\nand stabilize the net public debt-to-GDP ratio at 49.6 percent over the medium term.19 On the\n18 Box 2. IMF, Mexico Article IV, November 2021.\n19 Considering the most updated fiscal scenario, Precriterios de política económica para el 2023.\nPage 14\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nexpenditure side, the budget envisions a re-prioritization of expenditures toward health (a 27.6 percent\nincrease in real terms compared to 2021) and social assistance programs (real increment of 12.8 percent)\nto vulnerable groups while keeping public investment at 3.1 percent of GDP, mainly for flagship projects.\nThe impact of the pandemic-induced economic downturn in 2020 on debt is not expected to affect the\ndebt consolidation process, based on a contained fiscal deficit under the long-standing prudent fiscal\npolicy of Mexico, further supported by the limited fiscal impulse to remove. The budget envisages\nincreases in public revenues driven by the recovery in economic activity and efforts to tackle elusion and\nevasion. Also, to simplify the tax filing process, reducing costs, and increasing the tax base, the new budget\nlaw adopts (a) a simplified tax filing regime for independent workers that limits deductions and (b) a\nspecial regime for small firms with accelerated depreciation to boost investment and employment.\nTable 1. The operation also supported remote bank-account opening for Mexican migrants and expansion of financial services through electronic payment systems.", "question_ids": ["5B.3", "5B.4", "8B.2", "8B.6"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The legal actions were adopted in 2021–2022, with results targets for December 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The reforms were government measures supported by the López Obrador administration and implemented through SHCP and financial regulators.", "limitations": ["The source reports intended results and risks, not verified achievement of the 2023 targets."], "start_unit": 26, "end_unit": 29, "locator": "page 26 through page 29", "exact_excerpt": "The World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\ninflation forecast in 2022 to 6 percent. Banxico has also updated its projections for annual inflation in 2022\nfrom 5.2 percent to 6.4 percent.\nFiscal stance. Mexico's fiscal position is expected to improve through increased oil revenues. However, to\nmitigate additional inflationary pressures, the government relies on the revenue increase to cut excise tax\non gas and lower the income tax and VAT on gasoline distributors. World Bank’s staff analysis projects\nthat the windfall is expected to increase PEMEX revenues by around 0.8 percent of GDP. The federal\ngovernment is expected to increase revenues by 0.3 percent of GDP with tax exemption cost estimated\nto be 0.6 percent of GDP, totaling a net cost of 0.25 percent of GDP for the federal government (and a 0.5\npercent net benefit for the public sector).\nPoverty and distribution. The cost of the basic food basket increased by 13.9 (12.8) percent in rural\n(urban) areas in the 12 months to end-February, significantly above the average food inflation observed\nin LAC (9 percent). Food expenditures represent more than 50 percent of total expenditures of the bottom\n15 percent of the distribution and more than 40 percent of total expenditures of the bottom 60 percent\nof the distribution. As such, the purchasing power of poor and vulnerable households will be affected,\npotentially leading to heightened food insecurity, particularly in urban areas, where the largest number\nof poor reside.\n2.3. IMF RELATIONS\n24. On November 3, 2021, the Executive Board of the IMF considered the staff report that\nconcluded the Article IV consultation with Mexico. A two-year precautionary FCL arrangement for SDR\n44.6 billion (about US$61 billion, at the time of signing) was approved on November 22, 2019. The\narrangement was renewed for two years to an initial amount equivalent to SDR 35.7 billion (about US$50\nbillion at the time of signing) that will be phased out at the request of the Mexican authorities as the risks\ncontinue to decline. The IMF and World Bank maintain close collaboration on macroeconomic and\nstructural issues.\n3. GOVERNMENT PROGRAM\n25. The National Development Plan (NDP) 2018–2024 includes the programs and public policies\nreaffirming the GoM’s commitment to sustainable development and maintaining a sustainable fiscal\nframework. The NDP seeks to relaunch economic growth, anchored on formal jobs creation, by boosting\nemployment generation, improving education, health, and the population's overall well-being. It also\npursues balancing economic growth with regional integration and regional economic convergence. The\nNDP emphasizes interventions that benefit the most vulnerable groups, particularly the poor and the\nunderserved population in rural areas, and policies and programs to achieve the government’s target of\nreducing poverty.\n26. As the government starts the second half of its six-year mandate, the 2022 public budget aims\nto consolidate the economic recovery following the impact of the pandemic by increasing and\nPage 22\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nredirecting expenditures towards health, social programs, sustainable rural development, and public\ninvestment in flagship projects. The 2022 public budget24 aims to prioritize and contain spending while\nincreasing revenue resulting from the economic recovery and growing tax collection, partly due to the\nsimplified tax regime supported by this operation, and higher oil prices. The budget's focus is to prop up\nthe economic recovery by boosting social protection and public health systems and increasing public\ninvestment in flagship projects, particularly in the South and Southeast regions of the country, to improve\njob creation, GDP growth and productivity convergence with the rest of the country. The government has\nalso adopted a new simplified regime for low-income earners to strengthen the tax authority to combat\nevasion and avoidance, a critical structural challenge in Mexico. The reform is designed to be revenue-\nneutral in the short term to support the economic recovery and positively impact revenues in the medium\nterm once the recovery occurs due to an increase in formalization resulting from decreasing compliance\ncosts. In addition, Mexico increased the environmental expense budget by 30 percent from 2021 to 2022,\nensuring that sustainable forest management and climate change adaptation policies are integrated into\nMexico’s efforts to boost economic growth, enhance income opportunities, and improve livelihoods for\nurban and rural populations.\n4. PROPOSED OPERATION\n4.1. LINK TO GOVERNMENT PROGRAM AND OPERATION DESCRIPTION\n27. The development objective of the proposed operation is to support policy measures of the\nauthorities that: (a) support vulnerable groups and regions to reap the benefits of economic recovery;\nand (b) reform key policies and institutions that enable sustainable economic growth.\n28. The operation is structured around two pillars. Pillar 1 supports: (i) adopting a new, simplified\nregime for MSMEs and self-employed individuals; and (ii) policies and institutional reforms to facilitate\nfinancial inclusion, particularly for Mexican migrants in the United States. Pillar 2 supports reforms that:\n(i) improve the management of climate-induce disasters; (ii) ensure sustainable management and\nconservation of forests; (iii) enhance conservation areas that mutually store carbon and improve\nresilience; and (iv) enhance the voice and agency of vulnerable rural populations around natural resource\nmanagement.\n29. The operation is aligned with the government program. The operation supports key priorities\nadopted by the government in the 2022 public budget, including the simplified tax regime and the\nfinancial mechanisms to ensure support in case of natural disasters. It also supports key government\npriorities, such as strengthening the forestry law to bring additional regulatory certainty and regulations\nto increase financial inclusion. These reforms are expected to benefit the poorest rural areas contributing\nto closing regional income gaps, one of the main objectives of the government as stated in the NDP.\n24\nhttps://www.finanzaspublicas.hacienda.gob.mx/work/models/Finanzas_Publicas/docs/paquete_economico/cgpe/cgpe_2022.p\ndf.\nPage 23\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\n30. The design of this DPL includes lessons learned from prior policy operations. Recent DPFs in\nMexico show that stand-alone DPFs provide the needed flexibility for supporting reforms led by the\nGovernment in stages, as it builds consensus along the process. DPFs in Mexico have proven more\nimpactful when complemented by a program of technical and analytical tools that help build support\naround evidence of the reforms and ensure that the World Bank's analytical work is closely aligned to\nMexico’s demands and reform momentum. The analytical underpinnings of this operation have been\nbased on the broad Technical assistance (TA) and analytical work carried out in the recent past. TA and\nanalytical work are expected to continue in order to support policy implementation under the operation.\n4.2. PRIOR ACTIONS, RESULTS AND ANALYTICAL UNDERPINNINGS\nPillar 1: Inclusive economic growth\n31. Pillar 1 of the operation focuses on reforms supporting vulnerable groups and regions to better\nreap the benefits of economic growth, deepening reforms in long-standing areas of engagement with\nthe GoM. It will do so by (PA 1) simplifying the declaration and payment of taxes for MSMEs, (PA 2)\nfacilitating the identity verification of Mexicans in the US to open bank accounts in Mexico, and (PA 3)\nexpanding the supply of financial services through electronic payment systems.\nPrior Action 1: : The Borrower has enacted an amendment to the Income Tax Law to: (a) strengthen the\ntax framework by closing loopholes for tax evasion and avoidance, providing the tax authority with new\ntools to combat tax evasion and avoidance, including in mergers, acquisitions and restructurings; and (b)\nintroduce new simplified regimes for enterprises with income up to an amount of 35 million Mexican Pesos\nand self-employed, reducing the number of procedures to declare and pay taxes, as evidenced,\nrespectively, by the following provisions under the amendment to the Income Tax Law published in the\nOfficial Gazette on November 12, 2021: (i) amendments to Articles 24 and 161 of the Income Tax Law; and\n(ii) introduction of articles 113-E to 113-J under Title IV, Chapter II, Section IV, called ‘Simplified Trust\nRegime’, and Articles 206 to 215 under Title VII, Chapter XII, called “Simplified Trust Regime for legal\npersons” in the Income Tax Law.\n32. Rationale. Mexico has multiple tax regimes with significant administrative burdens that deter\nformalization, especially for small taxpayers. In Mexico, enterprises require 241 hours a year on average\nto comply with their tax obligations, compared to 161 hours in OECD member countries. Furthermore,\nthe Tax Incorporation Regime (Régimen de Incorporación Fiscal, RIF) introduced in 2014 was not effective\nin increasing formality or helping business growth. There are 2.1 million taxpayers, individuals, and micro\nand small establishments in Mexico subject to the same obligations as large companies, raising\nadministrative costs. As a result, tax avoidance and evasion are widespread in Mexico. Several studies\nshow that tax evasion is estimated to be between 2.6 and 6.2 percent of GDP.25\n33. Substance of the prior action. The reform aims to reduce the procedures and time allocated to\ndeclare and pay taxes in Mexico to increase tax compliance and facilitate formalization. Implementing\nmeasures to reduce informality has become particularly urgent as the share of informal workers has\nincreased. The reform seeks to reduce the cost of compliance for taxpayers with less administrative\ncapacity. The new simplified regime aims to maintain similar effective rates for income tax for individuals\n25 Buehn and Schneider 2016; San Martin et al. 2017.\nPage 24\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nwith lower incomes, calibrating the reform to ensure its revenue-neutrality in the short term. The new\nregime allows for the auto-population of the monthly tax returns based on e-invoices, significantly\nsimplifying tax filing for small and micro enterprises. Informality will be deterred by simplifying the\ncalculation, reducing the amount of taxes, and fighting the use of invoices—both false and real—currently\nused to deduct taxes. The reform simplifies the tax regimes for MSMEs and self-employed, and grants,\naccelerated depreciation to support investment. The RIF’s new simplified system for income tax extends\nto a broader set of taxpayers (i.e., professionals) for individuals with a gross income of up to MXN 3.5\nmillion per year (around US$170,000) and legal entities with an income of up to MXN 35 million annual\ngross income (around US$1.7 million). The new regime simplifies compliance with tax obligations,\npreventing the taxpayer from keeping all the digital tax receipts or invoices, since the tax collection will\nbe calculated directly on the total income.\n34. Expected results. The reform is designed to be revenue-neutral in the short term to support the\neconomy's recovery. Still, it is expected to raise revenues once the economic recovery is well established,\npartly due to the increase in formalization associated with the reduction in compliance costs.26\nInternational experience suggests that revenue gains can be large; for instance, in India, Argentina,\nSwitzerland, Chile, and Canada, similar reforms increased 15–30 percent in the taxpayer base over two to\nthree years. The indicator proposed tracks the reform's impact during the timeline of the operation and\nreduces the number of procedures to comply with tax payment obligations from 8 or 10 in 2021 to 6 in\n2022, depending on the taxpayer´s activity, therefore the net reduction in the tax procedures would be\nequivalent to 2 or 4, respectively. The new regime is expected to increase the taxpayer base above GDP\ngrowth in the medium to long term.27 Also, the reform will improve tax administration by strengthening\ntax enforcement and simplifying tax compliance. The SAT (Mexico’s Internal Revenue Service) will have\nnew monitoring tools to combat tax evasion and avoidance, among others, during mergers, acquisitions,\nand restructurings.28 Finally, by reducing informality in the long term, this reform will also lead to higher\nsocial security contributions.\nPrior Action 2: The Borrower's Securities and Banking Commission has amended provisions applicable to\ncredit institutions to facilitate financial inclusion for Mexican migrants in the United States and improve\nthe safety and affordability of remittances flows by enabling them to remotely open transaction accounts\nwith Mexican development and commercial banks and including additional credentials to be used as valid\ndocuments to prove their identity, as evidenced by the resolution issued by the Borrower’s Securities and\nBanking Commission amending general provisions applicable to credit institutions, published in the Official\nGazette on May 21, 2021.\n26 Materializing the full impact of this reform may require policy makers to act on several fronts at the same time, including\nsimplifying related areas (i.e. labor market regulations) in order to tilt the cost–benefit analysis of firms toward formality by\ncombining both positive and negative incentives.\n27 Most of these will come from a reduction of tax expenditures associated with discounts of personal income taxes, value added\ntax (VAT), and the special tax on production and services (IEPS) benefiting taxpayers currently filing taxes under the RIF, which\nrepresented MXN 13.5 billion (MMP) in 2021. The approved modifications of personal deduction due to donations, which\naccounted for a MXN 1.1 billion in 2021, are also expected to be reduced by around 30 percent.\n28 For example, in case of identifying a corporate restructuring or a merger whose only reason is the reduction of tax obligations\n(without a business reason), the legal classification could be changed to an alienation of goods. Also, the reform modifies\nobligations for natural and legal persons residing abroad. Among these is the obligation to present information on transfer prices\nto people with a source of wealth located in the national territory.\nPage 25", "excerpt_sha256": "1554544bd99c10562afa12873b9f0f4448cca2aa1f5b529a782461ca7416b6c7", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["Removed unsupported claims about neutral poverty and social effects, consumer protection, and cybersecurity."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0016-E013", "source_id": "SRC-684988a395d1a128", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B2B9230B7-CC63-5B20-E173-F5E94D116BC9%7D.pdf", "title": "IMF Country Report No. 23/356 — Mexico", "publisher": "Banco de México / International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "f93592f437a264c95c4c753720221642f0dd636d55e012cef97b95d1b59a294a", "fact_summary": "The IMF reported broad-based 2023 expansion, projected real GDP growth of 3.2%, unemployment of 2.7%, declining inflation, and resilient banking. Growth is expected to be\n3.2 percent in 2023, led by robust private consumption and investment, with notable strength\nin services and construction sector, and in auto production. The unemployment rate has fallen\nto 2.7 percent. Proactive monetary policy and a decline in global commodity prices are\nfacilitating disinflation. Economic activity is projected to slow to 2.1 percent in 2024. Although\nfiscal policy is expected to loosen next year, its impact on growth will be blunted by binding\ncapacity constraints, a continuation of tight monetary policy, and slowing growth in the U.S.\n\nThe authorities are projected to meet their 2023 fiscal targets. More restrained capital\nspending is expected to more than offset the lower tax revenue, especially on the VAT,\nyielding an overall deficit of 3.9 percent of GDP. This should result in a decline of gross public\nsector debt (by staff’s definition) to 52.7 percent of GDP in 2023. Banxico has held its policy\nrate at 11.25 percent since March 2023 and stated it will keep rate on hold for an extended\nperiod. With inflation and long-run inflation expectations in check, the real rate is now firmly in\ncontractionary territory.\n\nThe banking sector has strong capital positions and, as of May 2023, nonperforming loans are\nclose to record lows at 2.2 percent of total loans. Higher freight costs, strong domestic\ndemand, and adverse price developments increased the current account deficit slightly in\n2022 to 1.2 percent of GDP. Higher demand-driven imports are expected to widen the deficit\nfurther in 2023 to 1.5 percent of GDP. International reserves remain at comfortable levels.\n\nExecutive Board Assessment2\n\nExecutive Directors agreed with the thrust of the staff appraisal. They noted that the\nauthorities’ very strong policies and policy frameworks were instrumental in restraining public\ndebt and containing inflation, while achieving a broad-based economic expansion supported\nby robust private consumption and investment. Recognizing the opportunities for Mexico over\nthe medium term, Directors noted that securing sustainable and inclusive growth will require\ncontinuing with a sound macroeconomic policy mix, accompanied by a broad set of structural\nreforms to address the existing bottlenecks and make the economy more climate resilient.\n\n\n\n\n1 Under Article IV of the IMF's Articles of Agreement, the IMF holds bilateral discussions with members, usually every year. A staff\nteam visits the country, collects economic and financial information, and discusses with officials the country's economic developments\nand policies. On return to headquarters, the staff prepares a report, which forms the basis for discussion by the Executive Board.\n2 At the conclusion of the discussion, the Managing Director, as Chairman of the Board, summarizes the views of Executive Directors,\nand this summary is transmitted to the country's authorities. An explanation of any qualifiers used in summings up can be found here:\nhttp://www.IMF.org/external/np/sec/misc/qualifiers.htm.\n\nDirectors generally cautioned against an overly procyclical near-term fiscal stance and\nunderscored that decisive measures will be needed in 2025 and beyond to preserve fiscal\nsustainability over the medium term, while highlighting Mexico’s strong track record of meeting\nfiscal targets. Going forward, Directors emphasized the need to boost non-oil revenues, which\nremain below Latin American and OECD peers, noting that higher fiscal space will create\nroom for targeted social and infrastructure spending. They also saw scope to reform the\nmedium-term fiscal framework to increase its flexibility and credibility. Directors agreed that\ngreater transparency in fiscal reporting will improve accountability and welcomed the\ntransparent recording of support to Pemex in 2024 budget, while emphasizing the importance\nof ensuring the company’s commercial viability.\n\nDirectors agreed that the proactive approach of Banco de México to monetary policy has been\ninstrumental in containing inflationary pressures and ensuring that inflation expectations\nremain well-anchored. In the face of upside risks to inflation, they recommended caution in\nreducing the policy rate before inflation is on a clearer downward path toward the target.\nDirectors also underscored the importance of continuing to enhance communication practices.\nThey agreed that the flexible exchange rate should continue to be the key tool to facilitate\nadjustment to external and domestic shocks.\n\nDirectors agreed that the financial system remains resilient, with high capital and liquidity\nbuffers. They looked forward to continued implementation of key policy recommendations of\nthe 2022 Financial Sector Assessment Program. Directors emphasized the need to address\noutstanding gaps in the AML/CFT framework and enhance collaboration between various\nAML/CFT agencies and anti-corruption bodies. They also welcomed Mexico volunteering for\nan assessment of the transnational aspects of corruption.\n\nDirectors underscored the importance of supply side reforms to improve potential growth and\nraise living standards, including by taking advantage of the diversification of global supply\nchains. Given the significant gender gaps, they emphasized that these reforms should\ncomprise policies to further boost female labor force participation and remove legal\nimpediments to female economic empowerment. Better tackling corruption and crime,\nexpanding financial inclusion, as well as improving infrastructure and streamlining regulations\nwill also be key.\n\nDirectors agreed that a comprehensive and well-sequenced climate change strategy can\nprovide more durable sources of energy. Given the long-term risk of a reduction in global\ndemand for hydrocarbons, they encouraged switching to low carbon and renewable sources of\ngeneration, including by considering increasing the price of carbon.\n\nIt is expected that the next Article IV Consultation with Mexico will be held on the standard 12-\nmonth cycle.", "excerpt_sha256": "50ae24657b1c8e1e1ea7c12a121b82b78cacdf1f9c1417d147f84db074c960fa", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0016-E015", "source_id": "SRC-684988a395d1a128", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B2B9230B7-CC63-5B20-E173-F5E94D116BC9%7D.pdf", "title": "IMF Country Report No. 23/356 — Mexico", "publisher": "Banco de México / International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "f93592f437a264c95c4c753720221642f0dd636d55e012cef97b95d1b59a294a", "fact_summary": "The IMF judged the proposed 2024 budget procyclical, with a 2.4% of GDP fiscal impulse and a projected 5.4% deficit while the economy was above potential and inflation remained above target. The 2024 budget is procyclical, proposing a significant expansion in the fiscal position.\nWith a fiscal impulse of 2.4 percent of GDP, the fiscal stance for 2024 is unhelpfully procyclical at a\ntime when the economy is above potential and inflation is not yet back to target. The budget\ncombines a realistic revenue forecast with sizable increases in current spending items—notably for\nwages, pensions, and social spending—resulting in a deficit of 5.4 percent of GDP. The medium-\nterm budget framework envisages a large consolidation in 2025. While the authorities have a track\nrecord of meeting targets, achieving the 2025 fiscal target would necessitate additional measures of\naround 1.7 percent of GDP. A more prudent fiscal stance in 2024 would have been desirable—to\navoid creating the conditions for higher interest rates, a stronger currency, and a higher debt-to-\nGDP ratio. The current fiscal stance puts additional burden on monetary policy in achieving the\ntargeted disinflation. Under current policies, public debt would rise to 54.7 percent of GDP at end-\n2024 and stabilize at around 56 percent over the medium-term, conditional on the needed\nadjustment over the medium term. Public debt is assessed to be sustainable with high probability,\nwith fiscal risks mitigated by the country access to international and domestic debt markets.\n\n21.     The explicit inclusion of support for Pemex in the budget increases transparency and\nshould help facilitate a discussion of supporting the company versus other budgetary\npriorities. However, continued budgetary support to Pemex should be conditioned on a credible\nplan to ensure Pemex’s commercial viability. Furthermore, Mexico’s public sector contains many\nother entities that are distinct from the Federal government and the complex financial transactions\nbetween public sector entities—including through trust funds, state-affiliated enterprises, and\ndevelopment corporations—argues for a better reporting of consolidated fiscal outturns that is\naligned with international accounting practices. Such an improvement in statistics would allow for\nbetter appreciation of the trade-offs among competing demands on public resources as well as help\nidentify the size of potential fiscal risks and contingent liabilities.\n\n22. A stronger medium-term fiscal framework would increase the credibility of fiscal\npolicy and create greater fiscal space to respond to downside risks. A framework that more\nclearly links the desired public debt level to annual budgetary decisions would greatly increase the\ntransparency and accountability of fiscal policymaking. A broader revamp of the fiscal framework\nalong these lines could include: (i) a well-calibrated debt anchor, (ii) introducing a credible medium-\nterm budget framework; (iii) allowing for more countercyclical fiscal policy, potentially through the\nbuild-up of additional fiscal buffers; (iv) clarifying escape clauses from the fiscal rules and limiting\nthem to specified exceptional circumstances; (v) increasing the analysis of the impact of policies; and\n(vi) improving fiscal forecasting capacity.\n\n24.      Targeted fiscal policies would help address gender gaps. The authorities have increased\nefforts to boost gender equality but would benefit from: (i) gender budgeting, with an integration of\ngender policies into the budget through a Gender Impact Assessment; (ii) better collection of\ngender-related data on social and economic outturns; (iii) increasing support for maternal health\nand childcare; (iv) improving allocations to the most effective social protection programs; (v) more\ntargeted education support programs; (vi) abolishing the minimum contribution requirement for\nfunded pensions; and (vii) adopting gender-sensitive employment practices in the public sector.\n\n25.      Authorities’ views. The 2024 budget submitted to Congress is looser than previously\nplanned. However, this stems from an increased expenditure related to the current administration’s\ncommitments—principally, pensions, wages, and healthcare reforms. Additionally, capital\nexpenditure to complete key infrastructure projects will help Mexico seize the opportunity of\nnearshoring and boost connectivity and growth in the developing southern region. Needed\nconsolidation in 2025, beyond the expiration of one-off capital expenses, could be met with\ncontinued improvements in tax efficiency and normalization of monetary policy. Pemex has been\nincluded in the budget to improve transparency, support its cash position, and guarantee its debt\npayments. The resources transferred to Pemex change the cash position but neither affect the\noverall public sector balance nor hinder the Federal Government expenditure, as the transfers\nrequire Pemex’s balance improvement by the same amount. The current administration intends not\nto raise tax rates. Measures are being undertaken to address gender gaps, including plans to\nimprove government provision of childcare.\n\nB. Durably Bringing Inflation Back to Target\n\n26.      A proactive approach to monetary policy has helped contain inflationary pressures.\nBanxico was one of the first major central banks to begin a tightening cycle, raising its policy rate\nfrom 4 percent in June 2021 to 11.25 percent in March 2023. The real rate is now firmly in\ncontractionary territory. Inflation expectations have remained well-anchored, underscoring the\ncredibility of the inflation targeting regime.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND          11", "excerpt_sha256": "b1b320b421002ef5926ca2d38448b864ab27dd87f949cdf52f151506c4394bb1", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0016-E020", "source_id": "SRC-684988a395d1a128", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B2B9230B7-CC63-5B20-E173-F5E94D116BC9%7D.pdf", "title": "IMF Country Report No. 23/356 — Mexico", "publisher": "Banco de México / International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "f93592f437a264c95c4c753720221642f0dd636d55e012cef97b95d1b59a294a", "fact_summary": "The FSAP implementation table reported no measures taken on regulatory-agency autonomy, organizational resources, consolidated supervision, and refinement of risk-based supervision. The IMF recommended stronger coordination, resources, sanctions, asset recovery, and verification of beneficial ownership in procurement.", "question_ids": ["5B.5", "5B.7", "7B.10", "8B.6"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The findings concern the institutional and enforcement status reviewed in 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The report evaluates Mexico's anti-corruption institutions and authorities during AMLO's administration; it does not establish personal enrichment by López Obrador.", "limitations": ["The evidence does not prove that AMLO personally directed corruption or benefited from it."], "start_unit": 88, "end_unit": 92, "locator": "page 88 through page 92", "exact_excerpt": "MEXICO\n\n\n\n     Annex VIII. Leveraging the Anti-Money Laundering Efforts to\n                   Support Anti-Corruption Efforts1\n\nA. Interaction between Mexico’s AML and AC Frameworks\n\n1.      Corruption and money laundering (ML) are symbiotic as they tend to co-occur. The\npresence of one tends to create and reciprocally reinforce the incidence of the other. 2 The strongest\nlink between the two crimes is the need for corruption proceeds to be laundered by the criminals,\nwhich implicates the AML framework. Some of the AML measures—such as the enhanced due\ndiligence measures applicable to Politically Exposed Persons (PEPs) that pose high risk for corruption\nand the filing of Suspicious Transaction Reports (STRs) by reportable entities—could, in turn, be\nhelpful in detecting and preventing corruption in the first place. Furthermore, corruption could\nundermine the proper functioning and effectiveness of the AML/CFT framework (e.g., independence\nand governance of AML/CFT institutions). It is therefore important that frameworks aimed at\ntackling corruption and money laundering work in tandem to tackle financial crimes and limit their\nimpact on the macroeconomy to help ensure a sustainable and inclusive economic growth and\npromote a conducive investment environment.\n\n2.      The 2020 National Risk Assessment on ML and terrorism financing ranked laundering\nof corruption proceeds among the highest risks in Mexico. The preliminary results from the most\nrecent update to the National Risk Assessment confirm that corruption remains among the highest\nML threats. Therefore, it is particularly relevant in the context of Mexico to further refine the\nunderstanding of risks of laundering of domestic corruption to highlight the key sectors at risk and\nthe macroeconomic impact of those crimes. Such understanding of risks of corruption and\nlaundering of its proceeds could help better inform policy determination under the AML and AC\nframeworks and ensure their better integration and complementarity.\n\n3.      The recognition of corruption as a substantial problem in Mexico led to the 2015\nreforms. The National Anti-Corruption System (Sistema Nacional Anticorrupción or SNA) was\nintroduced following amendments to the constitution, along with a package of new laws and legal\namendments that provide for the regulatory and institutional framework in the fight against\ncorruption. 3 The SNA is coordinated by the SNA Coordination Committee comprising seven\n\n1 Prepared by Adrian Wardzynski, with research support from Alessandro D’Amelio (both LEG).\n\n2 Countries that struggle to tackle take decisive actions against corruption usually have lower levels of compliance\n\nwith AML standards. See C. Verdugo, Compliance with the AML/CFT International Standard: Lessons from a Cross-\nCountry Analysis, IMF Working Paper, WP/11/177.\n3 In addition to changes to Article 113 of the Constitution, the AC legal package included the General Law on the\n\nNational Anti-Corruption System (Ley General del Sistema Nacional Anticorrupción), the General Law on\nAdministrative Responsibilities (Ley General de Responsabilidades Administrativas), the Law of Control and\nAccountability of the Federation (Ley de Fiscalización y Rendición de Cuentas de la Federación), amendments to the\nOrganic Law of the Federal Court of Administrative Justice (Ley Orgánica del Tribunal Federal de Justicia\nAdministrativa), amendments to the Organic Law of the Attorney’s General Office (Ley Orgánica de la Fiscalía General\nde la República), amendments to the Federal Penal Code (Código Penal Federal), amendments to the Organic Law of\nFederal Public Administration (Ley Orgánica de la Administración Pública Federal).\n\n\n84   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nnational institutions. The Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU) and the AML supervisors, i.e., the National\nBanking and Securities Commission (Comisión Nacional Bancaria y de Valores or CNBV) and the tax\nauthority (Servicio de Administración Tributaria or SAT), are indirectly represented at the Committee\nthrough the Ministry of Public Administration (MPA).\n\n4.      Policies of the SNA could reflect better the high risk of laundering of proceeds of\ncorruption. The SNA Coordination Committee approved the National Anti-Corruption Policy\n(Prioridades e implementación de la Política Nacional or PNA) in January 2020 and the National\nImplementation Plan in January 2022. The overall effectiveness of the PNA will also depend on the\nstrength of Mexico’s AML framework, especially given that corruption is among the highest ML\nthreats in Mexico. As part of operationalization of these policies, the priorities for the PNA and its\nimplementation plan benchmarks should reflect AML measures and close coordination with AML\nauthorities.\n\n5.      Coordinating policy priorities for AML and AC could achieve better outcomes. Such\ncoordination could ensure common understanding of risks of corruption and ML and the\nvulnerabilities of different sectors for misuse, better allocation of resources, and prioritization of\nboth frameworks to achieve common outcomes. At the operational level, the MPA, FIU, and AML\nsupervisors should exchange information and cooperate by drawing on the synergies between their\nmandates and relevant information which would enable taking joint action against corruption and\nits proceeds. Given the importance of financial intelligence produced by the FIU to detect and\ndisrupt proceeds of corruption, as well as the role of banks and other financial and non-financial\ngatekeepers in preventing access of “dirty money” into the formal and informal economy, it is\nimportant that the AML and AC policies are coordinated to ensure that the mutually reinforcing\neffects of the AML and AC frameworks are fully utilized.\n\nB. Laundering of Proceeds of Corruption and AML Preventative Measures\n\n6.      PEPs are subject to enhanced due diligence under the AML framework in Mexico. The\nAML financial sector supervisor in Mexico, the CNBV, has dedicated criteria that it uses to verify\nwhether banks and other financial institutions under its supervisory purview are compliant with the\napplicable preventive measures for PEPs. The CNBV has also published guidance to financial entities\non the risks of corruption and related money laundering, which includes details on the necessary\ncontrols related to PEPs. There is also a list of PEPs published by the Ministry of Finance and Public\nCredit to further aid compliance by regulated entities.\n\n7.       The implementation of AML controls applicable to PEPs could be further strengthened\nand the major gaps in the legal framework of the non-financial sector addressed. The CNBV\nfound numerous non-compliance issues in enhanced due diligence processes applied by financial\ninstitutions in relation to PEPs. These include inadequate risk assessment and monitoring of\ntransactions to ensure alignment with the roles, level, and responsibilities of PEPs, failures to\ndetermine PEPs that may be controlling legal entities, reliance on outdated lists of PEPs, as well as\nother documentation and quality of information issues. While these are primarily about aspects of\n\n\n\n                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND       85\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\neffective implementation, the regulatory framework in the non-financial sector is not yet fully in\nplace. Currently, the Designated Non-Financial Businesses and Professions (DNFBPs)—such as\nlawyers, notaries, accountants, real estate agents, or Trust and Company Service Providers—are not\nlegally obliged under Mexico’s AML legal framework to carry out due diligence checks in relation to\nPEPs. 4 This is particularly important given the key role that such gatekeepers play in laundering\nproceeds of corruption through activities such as running and managing of business activities of\nlegal entities or purchasing of expensive real estate, among the most common techniques to\nlaunder corruption proceeds in Mexico.\n\n8.      STRs filed by the AML regulated entities are an important aspect of detecting financial\ncrimes, including corruption. The FIU indicated that it receives more than 340,000 STRs per year.\nThese cover suspicions of money laundering and its underlying financial crimes such as corruption\nor tax crimes.\n\n9.       Mexico's legal framework for STRs needs significant improvements, especially in\nrelation to DNFBPs. Most of the technical deficiencies in relation to the STRs identified in the\nFinancial Action Task Force (FATF) Mutual Evaluation Report published in 2018 remain unresolved. 5\nIn the case of the STRs obligations applicable to financial institutions, there are issues with the\napplicable timeframes and the level of suspicion required to trigger filing. In the case of the DNFBPs,\nthe existing obligation to file Notices should be amended to ensure comprehensive coverage and\nalignment with the FATF Recommendations. This translates directly into the low levels of reports\nfiled by the DNFBPs and the inability to effectively sanction such practices. More broadly, the overall\nAML regulatory and supervisory framework over the DNFBPs requires substantial strengthening. 6\n\n10.      Mexico should address deficiencies in the implementation of the STR obligations and\nboost the capacity of the FIU to prioritize the STRs related to PEPs. In addition to addressing\nthe legal deficiencies, the implementation of STR obligations should be further strengthened. Based\non the supervisory activities of the CNBV, there are recurrent issues in the accuracy of the customer\ndata that can compromise the integrity and reliability of the client records and related filing\nobligations. Further deficiencies were found by the CNBV in the application of the minimum criteria\nfor the filing of reports under the “24-hours process” and the quality of information included in the\nSTRs (i.e., the description of the underlying operations and the reasons for the suspicions). The FIU\nshould further promote compliance with best practices on the filing of STRs, including those related\nto PEPs and suspicious activities related to laundering generated by corruption, and prioritize\nensuing compliance and raising awareness of the DNBFPs. Given the large number of reports\nreceived by the FIU, the authorities should also ensure that it has adequate resources, both human\nand technological, to carry out sufficient screening of the received reports while prioritizing the STRs\nrelated to PEPs.\n\n4 See the Financial Action Task Force Follow-up Report on Mexico published in May 2023 and available at\n\nhttps://www.fatf-gafi.org/content/dam/fatf-gafi/fur/Follow-Up-Report-Mexico-2023.pdf.coredownload.pdf.\n5 For the 2018 Mutual Evaluation Report, see https://www.fatf-gafi.org/en/publications/Mutualevaluations/Mer-\n\nmexico-2018.html.\n6 See the 2023 Follow-Up Report available at https://www.fatf-gafi.org/content/dam/fatf-gafi/fur/Follow-Up-Report-\n\nMexico-2023.pdf.coredownload.pdf.\n\n\n86   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\n11.      Misuse of legal entities is among the techniques most used in Mexico to launder\nproceeds of crimes, including corruption. Shell companies and front companies are commonly\nutilized to conceal the beneficial ownership of assets and funds as well as the true purpose of\ntransactions. These are also used by PEPs in Mexico to cover payment of bribes and embezzle public\nfunds.\n\n12.      Implementation of the AML framework in Mexico does not adequately ensure\navailability of beneficial ownership information. The AML framework requires that regulated\nentities identify beneficial ownership information as part of their customer due diligence processes.\nThere can be however doubts about the veracity of this source of beneficial ownership information\nin light of the effectiveness issues. 7 As identified by the CNBV, there are significant issues in the\nprocedures followed by financial institutions to identify beneficial owners including for PEPs.\nDeficiencies have been identified in the collection and review of the necessary data and\ndocumentation that is essential for this largely factual test. The difficulties in reliance on the\nbeneficial ownership information obtained through regulated entities were also acknowledged by\nthe FIU that requires it for the purposes of financial analysis and production of financial intelligence\nreports for law enforcement agencies. The prosecutors generally rely on their own analysis—\nincluding review of the legal structure, board minutes, and field-based investigations—for the\ndetermination of beneficial ownership information. Given the key value of beneficial ownership\ninformation in tackling financial crimes, the authorities should step up supervisory oversight and\napply proportional and dissuasive sanctions in cases of non-compliance. There are also significant\nissues in the legal framework applicable to the DNFBPs, including in relation to the understanding of\nthe legal and control structure of the customers, which should be addressed through legislative\nchanges.\n\n13.     The new beneficial ownership information requirements arising under the tax laws are\ncurrently underutilized for AML purposes. The amendments to the Federal Tax Code in\nNovember 2021, which took effect in January 2022, introduced new obligations pursuant to Article\n32-B Ter on legal entities and their service providers to obtain and report beneficial ownership\ninformation to the Mexican tax authorities (SAT). These new rules were introduced to enable the SAT\nto obtain such information upon request and then report to foreign tax authorities in compliance\nwith requirements arising under the international tax transparency standards overseen by the Global\nForum on Transparency and Exchange of Information. 8 While these may also be of value for the\nbroader entity transparency objectives and tackling of financial crimes, they have not been used to\ndate in this context. Moreover, given the specific rationale for the introduction of these new\nobligations, there is no specific database that can be consulted by other competent authorities and\nregulated entities at the SAT for the beneficial ownership information that it has the ability to\nrequest. The SAT also does not verify the information. Mexico should introduce a beneficial\n\n7 For an analysis regarding the overall low effectiveness of the AML preventive measures, including those relating to\n\nbeneficial ownership requirements, see the 2018 Mutual Evaluation Report of Mexico adopted by the FATF and\navailable at https://www.fatf-gafi.org/en/countries/detail/Mexico.html.\n8 See https://www.oecd.org/publications/global-forum-on-transparency-and-exchange-of-information-for-tax-\n\npurposes-mexico-2023-second-round-6fd9ab78-en.htm.\n\n\n\n                                                                            INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND            87\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nownership registry and establish relevant processes to ensure that the information collected is\naccurate, adequate, and up to date. Beneficial ownership information should also be required to be\ncollected and verified as part of the public procurement process.\n\nC. Prosecution of Money Laundering and Corruption\n\n14.      The jurisdiction and cooperation between the specialized prosecutor offices\nresponsible for money laundering and corruption in Mexico should be enhanced. Money\nlaundering cases are prosecuted by the Specialized Prosecutor Office for Organized Crime (Fiscalía\nEspecializada en Materia de Delincuencia Organizada or FEMDO). This office can also carry out\nparallel investigations of money laundering and other financial crimes, such as corruption, if they are\nconsidered of high impact. In 2019 the Specialized Prosecutor Office for Corruption (Fiscalía\nEspecializada en Combate a la Corrupción or FMCCO) was established. While this office was\nexpected to take the lead on corruption-related cases, it has mainly dealt with matters relating to\nabuse of office, which is one of the forms of corruption. Corruption and bribery cases are not among\nits top five categories of cases and it has not yet prosecuted any high-level corruption cases. The\nauthorities should clarify the jurisdiction between the FEMDO and FEMCCO and also provide for\ntheir close cooperation to avoid fragmentation that would adversely affect the effectiveness to\nprosecute corruption cases and related money laundering.\n\n15.     The authorities should ensure that there are strict AC policies applicable to the law\nenforcement authorities, at both federal and state levels, as well as the judiciary. The level of\ncorruption in Mexico can affect the functioning of the law enforcement system and undermines the\ncapacity to investigate and prosecute corruption and money laundering. The number prosecutions\nand convictions relating to corruption and money laundering is not commensurate with Mexico’s\nrisk profile. The authorities should take steps to increase resources, capacity, and expertise as well as\nensure adequate budget of the law enforcement authorities. Confiscation and asset recovery should\nbe seen as major policy objectives within the national AML and AC policies and implementation\nplans in order to ensure that crime does not pay.\n\n\n\n\n88   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND", "excerpt_sha256": "cbaeabc12eb75d780c6f0f351a804d89cb38d728fd290435b2a3739b815d8dca", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0017-E003", "source_id": "SRC-065783efb0b09db2", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B44F43B6B-61BD-D795-4561-1601FDD3FDB0%7D.pdf", "title": "Mexico: 2022 Article IV Consultation—Press Release and Staff Report", "publisher": "International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "8fac4146590dbe2ed458f7356748f5880d021e03cc4982b1e7efe083d33f622a", "fact_summary": "Fiscal reserves in Mexico’s stabilization fund had fallen below 0.1 percent of GDP after extensive pandemic use. Fiscal reserves\n    in the Fondo de Estabilización de los Ingresos Presupuestarios (FEIP, also known as ‘the\n    stabilization fund’) have fallen to less than 0.1 percent of GDP, given extensive usage during the\n    pandemic and a restrictive replenishment mechanism under the Federal Budget and Fiscal\n    Responsibility Law (FRBL). 8 This is a potential constraint on the government’s ability to respond\n    quickly to future shocks. The authorities estimate that a minimum of 0.3 percent of GDP would\n    provide sufficient resources for a fiscal policy response. Recent amendments to the FRBL\n    proposed by the authorities as part of the 2023 budget would bring FEIP reserves closer to this\n    desired level, by enabling utilization of headroom against the government’s debt ceiling. This\n    approach would enhance preparedness in the short-term. Such reforms could include\n    (Annex IV of Staff Report 2019): (i) a well-calibrated debt anchor; (ii) broader coverage of\n    expenditure in the structural spending rule; (iii) a medium-term fiscal strategy that specifies the\n    post-shock adjustment path to return to the debt target; (iv) tighter triggers for the use of\n    escape clauses; and (v) a strengthened role for the fiscal council.\n\n30.     A medium-term fiscal strategy could help to anchor expectations about future fiscal\npolicy. The public debt ratio is expected to remain broadly unchanged in the next decade. 9 Weaker-\nthan-expected growth or adjustment slippages would thus result in a rising debt path. Nonetheless,\nthe Sovereign Risk and Debt Sustainability Analysis (SRDSA) (Annex III) indicates that public debt\nremains sustainable. With the proposed fiscal framework reform, a credible debt anchor and a\nmedium-term fiscal strategy could increase the scope to let fiscal policy act as a shock absorber and\nreduce the volatility in activity as a result.\n\nAuthorities’ Views\n\n31.     The authorities see advantages to maintaining the current retail fuel price stabilization\nmechanism. Given the current administration’s pledge that fuel prices will not rise in real terms\nabove their November 2018 level and the legal basis of the pricing mechanism, the authorities\nprefer to continue with the current prompt and programmatic fuel price stabilization mechanism\nrather than more targeted support.\n\n8 Under Mexico’s fiscal framework, FEIP has served as the de facto source of contingent financing for the budget\n\nalongside smaller trust funds when revenue shortfalls materialized during the 2020 downturn. These fiscal reserves\nare critical as Mexico’s fiscal framework does not permit the issuance of debt for non-capital spending. The current\nframework, enshrined in the FBFRL, also places restrictions on how FEIP can be replenished, with balance sheet\ntransfers to FEIP likely viewed as expenditures that must be balanced by a pre-identified revenue source or spending\ncuts elsewhere in the budget.\n9 The growth-adjusted interest factor, sometimes referred to as (r-g), captures the interest costs of the debt ratio,\n\nadjusted for growth effects, which, on their own, lower the latter. The authorities pointed to a track record of tapering\nsubsidies when market price pressures abated. Other policies addressing food prices include the\nremoval of tariffs on key food imports and regulatory agreements with retailers on food price\npassthrough. However, in anticipation of possible downside risks, they are interested in reforms to\nthe fiscal framework that would enable the allocation of fiscal savings and funding from debt\nissuance to the stabilization fund, while respecting key tenets of the current framework. In case of a\nshock affecting vulnerable population, the authorities consider they can create space to provide\nsupport by reallocating spending away from public investment.\n\nExternal Sector Policies\n\n32.
